Values of det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , m , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,m,d} The following theorem is a consequence of Theorem 3.22 and allows us to determine det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} from determinants of the blocks 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π {\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi} for reduced pairs ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) . Theorem 7.1 . For all m , n , d ∈ ℕ m,n,d\in{\mathbb{N}} we have the formula det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d = ∏ ( σ , π ) ( det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π ) N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) , \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d}=\prod_{(\sigma,\pi)}(\det{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi})^{N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d)}, where the product is over nonzero reduced pairs ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) of degree d ′ ≤ d d^{\prime}\leq d and N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) is as in Theorem 3.22 . Furthermore, when m = n m=n this formula simplifies to det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d = ∏ σ det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , σ , \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d}=\prod_{\sigma}\det{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\sigma}, where the product is over σ \sigma such that ( σ , σ ) (\sigma,\sigma) is reduced of degree d ′ ≤ d d^{\prime}\leq d and A σ , π ​ ( d ) ​ ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) A_{\sigma,\pi}(d){m\choose\ell(\sigma)} is odd. Proof. Since the determinant of a block diagonal matrix is the product of the determinants of the blocks and the determinant of the 1 × 1 1\times 1 identity matrix is clearly 1 1 , the first formula is immediate from Theorem 3.22 . To derive the second formula from the first, note that since det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π = ± 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}=\pm 1 by Proposition 2.7 , ( det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π ) N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) (\det{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi})^{N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d)} is 1 1 if N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) is even and det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π \det{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi} otherwise. In the special case where m = n m=n , we have N σ , π ​ ( m , m , d ) := { 2 ​ A σ , π ​ ( d ) ​ ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) ​ ( m ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) if ​ σ ≠ π , A σ , π ​ ( d ) ​ ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) 2 if ​ σ = π . N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,m,d):=\begin{cases}2A_{\sigma,\pi}(d){m\choose\ell(\sigma)}{m\choose\ell(\pi)}&\text{if }\sigma\neq\pi,\\ A_{\sigma,\pi}(d){m\choose\ell(\sigma)}^{2}&\text{if }\sigma=\pi.\end{cases} Thus N σ , π ​ ( m , m , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,m,d) is odd if and only if σ = π \sigma=\pi and both A σ , π ​ ( d ) A_{\sigma,\pi}(d) and ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) {m\choose\ell(\sigma)} are odd. ∎ Remark 7.2 . When computing det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} for m = n m=n , the parity of A σ , σ ​ ( d ) A_{\sigma,\sigma}(d) is easy to check. Indeed, if ( σ , σ ) (\sigma,\sigma) is reduced of degree d ′ > 0 d^{\prime}>0 and ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) > 2 \ell(\sigma)>2 then g σ , σ ≤ 1 g_{\sigma,\sigma}\leq 1 , so we find that A σ , σ ​ ( d ) A_{\sigma,\sigma}(d) is congruent to δ d , d ′ \delta_{d,d^{\prime}} mod 2 2 (if ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) = 2 \ell(\sigma)=2 or g σ , σ = 0 g_{\sigma,\sigma}=0 ) or 1 1 (otherwise). Theorem 7.3 (Periodicity) . Fix d d and let r r be the least positive integer such that 2 r > d 2^{r}>d . Then det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} has period 2 r 2^{r} in both m m and n n , i.e., for all m m and n n we have det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d = det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m + 2 r , n , d = det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n + 2 r , d . \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d}=\det{\sf RSK}_{m+2^{r},n,d}=\det{\sf RSK}_{m,n+2^{r},d}. Proof. Let ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) be a nonzero reduced pair of degree d ′ ≤ d d^{\prime}\leq d and let f σ , π ​ ( m , n ) f_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n) be the function recording the parity of N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) . By the first formula in Theorem 7.1 and the fact that det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π = ± 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}=\pm 1 by Proposition 2.7 , it suffices to show that f σ , π ​ ( m , n ) f_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n) is periodic of period 2 r 2^{r} in both m m and n n . Recall from Theorem 3.22 that N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) := { A σ , π ​ ( d ) ​ ( ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) ​ ( n ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) + ( m ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) ​ ( n ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) ) if ​ σ ≠ π , A σ , π ​ ( d ) ​ ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) ​ ( n ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) if ​ σ = π . N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d):=\begin{cases}A_{\sigma,\pi}(d)\left({m\choose\ell(\sigma)}{n\choose\ell(\pi)}+{m\choose\ell(\pi)}{n\choose\ell(\sigma)}\right)&\text{if }\sigma\neq\pi,\\ A_{\sigma,\pi}(d){m\choose\ell(\sigma)}{n\choose\ell(\pi)}&\text{if }\sigma=\pi.\end{cases} Let f ~ σ , π ​ ( m , n ) := { ( ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) ​ ( n ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) + ( m ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) ​ ( n ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) ) ​ mod ​ 2 if ​ σ ≠ π , ( m ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ) ​ ( n ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) ​ mod ​ 2 if ​ σ = π . \widetilde{f}_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n):=\begin{cases}\left({m\choose\ell(\sigma)}{n\choose\ell(\pi)}+{m\choose\ell(\pi)}{n\choose\ell(\sigma)}\right)\text{ mod }2&\text{if }\sigma\neq\pi,\\ {m\choose\ell(\sigma)}{n\choose\ell(\pi)}\text{ mod }2&\text{if }\sigma=\pi.\end{cases} Since A σ , π ​ ( d ) A_{\sigma,\pi}(d) is independent of m m and n n , it suffices to show that f ~ σ , π ​ ( m , n ) \widetilde{f}_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n) has the desired periodicity. For all a , b ≥ 0 a,b\geq 0 , Lucas’s theorem implies a binomial coefficient ( a b ) {a\choose b} is odd if and only if the 1 1 ’s in the binary expansion of b b are a subset of the 1 1 ’s in the binary expansion of a a . When b b is fixed and has s s digits in its binary expansion, this mean that the parity of ( a b ) {a\choose b} depends only on the last s s digits of the binary expansion of a a . Hence the parity of ( a b ) {a\choose b} is a periodic function of period 2 s 2^{s} in a a . It follows that f ~ σ , π ​ ( m , n ) \widetilde{f}_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n) is periodic of period 2 s 2^{s} in both m m and n n , where s s is minimal such that 2 s > ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) , ℓ ⁡ ( π ) 2^{s}>\ell(\sigma),\ell(\pi) . Since the degree- d d reduced pair maximizing ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) \ell(\sigma) and ℓ ⁡ ( π ) \ell(\pi) is ( 1 d , 1 d ) (1^{d},1^{d}) , it follows that for any reduced pair ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) of degree d ′ ≤ d d^{\prime}\leq d , the function f ~ σ , π ​ ( m , n ) \widetilde{f}_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n) has period 2 r 2^{r} with r r as in the corollary statement. This completes the proof. ∎ Example 7.4 . By Theorem 7.1 , det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 2 k , 2 k , d = 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{2^{k},2^{k},d}=1 for any d < 2 k d<2^{k} . Indeed, by Lucas’s theorem, for each reduced weight pair ( σ , σ ) (\sigma,\sigma) , N σ , σ ​ ( 2 k , 2 k , d ) N_{\sigma,\sigma}(2^{k},2^{k},d) is even since ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) ≤ d < 2 k \ell(\sigma)\leq d<2^{k} . Example 7.5 . We fully determine det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} for d ≤ 3 d\leq 3 using Theorem 7.1 and Example 3.24 . The case d = 1 d=1 is easy: 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 1 = Id m ​ n {\sf RSK}_{m,n,1}={\rm Id}_{mn} so det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 1 = 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,1}=1 . When d = 2 d=2 , the only nontrivial block that occurs in 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 2 {\sf RSK}_{m,n,2} is 𝖱𝖲𝖪 11 , 11 {\sf RSK}_{11,11} , which has determinant − 1 -1 and appears with multiplicity N 11 , 11 ​ ( m , n , 2 ) = 1 ⋅ ( m 2 ) ​ ( n 2 ) N_{11,11}(m,n,2)=1\cdot{m\choose 2}{n\choose 2} . By evaluating the parity of these binomial coefficients via Lucas’s theorem, we compute det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 2 = { − 1 if ​ m , n ≡ 2 ​ or ​ 3 ​ ( mod ​ 4 ) , 1 otherwise . \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,2}=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if }m,n\equiv 2\text{ or }3\ (\text{mod }4),\\ 1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} When d = 3 d=3 , there are four nontrivial blocks that can occur in 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 3 {\sf RSK}_{m,n,3} , namely those indexed by the reduced pairs ( 11 , 11 ) (11,11) , ( 21,111 ) (21,111) , ( 12,111 ) (12,111) , and ( 111,111 ) (111,111) . Direct computation shows that det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 21,111 = det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 12,111 = 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{21,111}=\det{\sf RSK}_{12,111}=1 and det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 11 , 11 = det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 111,111 = − 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{11,11}=\det{\sf RSK}_{111,111}=-1 . Since A 11 , 11 ​ ( 3 ) = 4 A_{11,11}(3)=4 and A 111,111 ​ ( 3 ) = 1 A_{111,111}(3)=1 , we conclude that det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 3 = { − 1 if ​ m , n ≡ 3 ​ ( mod ​ 4 ) , 1 otherwise . \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,3}=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if }m,n\equiv 3\ (\text{mod }4),\\ 1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} The reader can compare these formulas to the first three columns of Table 2 . Remark 7.6 . The minimal period of det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} for a fixed d d may be lower than the value guaranteed in Theorem 7.3 . For instance, the proof of Theorem 7.3 demonstrates that det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 4 \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,4} has period 8 8 because the parity of N 1111 , 1111 ​ ( m , n , 4 ) N_{1111,1111}(m,n,4) is a function of minimal period 8 8 by Lucas’s theorem. This turns out to be irrelevant, since det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1111 , 1111 = 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{1111,1111}=1 . Thus the minimal period of det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 4 \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,4} is 4 4 rather than 8 8 . Example 7.7 . We fully determine det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 2 , 2 , d \det{\sf RSK}_{2,2,d} using Theorem 7.1 . By Theorem 3.22 , we know that the only nontrivial blocks appearing in 𝖱𝖲𝖪 2 , 2 , d {\sf RSK}_{2,2,d} are those corresponding to reduced pairs of the form ( a ​ a , a ​ a ) (aa,aa) for some a > 0 a>0 . The explicit description of the eigenvalues of 𝖱𝖲𝖪 a ​ a , a ​ a {\sf RSK}_{aa,aa} in Example shows that det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 a ​ a , a ​ a = − 1 \det{\sf RSK}_{aa,aa}=-1 if and only if ⌊ a + 1 2 ⌋ \lfloor\frac{a+1}{2}\rfloor is odd, which occurs if and only if a a is congruent to 1 1 or 2 2 mod 4 4 . Moreover, A a ​ a , a ​ a ​ ( d ) = 4 ​ ( d − 2 ​ a ) + δ d , 2 ​ a A_{aa,aa}(d)=4(d-2a)+\delta_{d,2a} is odd if and only if d = 2 ​ a d=2a , and ( m ℓ ⁡ ( a ​ a ) ) = ( 2 2 ) {m\choose\ell(aa)}={2\choose 2} is odd. Combining these facts shows that det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 2 , 2 , d = { − 1 if ​ d ≡ 2 , 4 ​ ( mod ​ 8 ) , 1 otherwise . \det{\sf RSK}_{2,2,d}=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if \ }d\equiv 2,4\ (\text{mod }8),\\ 1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} For any fixed d d , Theorem 7.1 allows one to in principle determine det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , m , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,m,d} by computing det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , σ \det{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\sigma} for a finite collection of weights σ \sigma and combining them via a (somewhat messy) periodic formula using the binary expression of m m . One can also compute det 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d \det{\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} in this manner, although in the general case the number of pairs ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) contributing to the formula is much greater than when m = n m=n . 8. More specifically, Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d = N 0 ​ ( m , n , d ) + ∑ ( σ , π ) N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) ​ Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π , {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d}=N_{0}(m,n,d)+\sum_{(\sigma,\pi)}N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d){\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}, where the sum is over nonzero reduced pairs ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) of degree d ′ ≤ d d^{\prime}\leq d and the expressions N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) and N 0 ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{0}(m,n,d) are as in Theorem 3.22 . The lead term of this polynomial is Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d ( d ! ) 2 ​ m d ​ n d \frac{{\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}}}{(d!)^{2}}m^{d}n^{d} whenever Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d ≠ 0 {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}}\neq 0 . Proof. The first formula in the theorem statement is immediate from the block decomposition of 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} given in Theorem 3.22 . The formula for N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) in Theorem 3.22 shows that for fixed d d , N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) is a polynomial in m m and n n whose lead term has total degree ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) + ℓ ⁡ ( π ) \ell(\sigma)+\ell(\pi) . Similarly, the expression for N 0 ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{0}(m,n,d) is a polynomial whose lead term has total degree d + 1 d+1 . It follows that Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} is polynomial in m m and n n for any fixed d d . Moreover, the term in the sum with the largest possible growth in m m and n n comes from the reduced pair ( 1 d , 1 d ) (1^{d},1^{d}) , which maximizes ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) \ell(\sigma) and ℓ ⁡ ( π ) \ell(\pi) . The lead term of N 1 d , 1 d ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{1^{d},1^{d}}(m,n,d) is ( m ​ n ) d ( d ! ) 2 \frac{(mn)^{d}}{(d!)^{2}} , and the result follows. ∎ Example 8.2 . We compute Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} for d ≤ 3 d\leq 3 using Theorem 8.1 and Example 3.24 . When d = 1 d=1 , we have Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 1 = m ​ n {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,1}=mn since 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 1 = Id m ​ n {\sf RSK}_{m,n,1}={\rm Id}_{mn} . When d = 2 d=2 , Theorem 8.1 and the values N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) from Example 3.24 give the formula Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 2 = N 0 ​ ( m , n , 2 ) + Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 11 , 11 ​ ( m 2 ) ​ ( n 2 ) . {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,2}=N_{0}(m,n,2)+{\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{11,11}{m\choose 2}{n\choose 2}. Evaluating N 0 ​ ( m , n , 2 ) N_{0}(m,n,2) and observing from Example 1.2 that Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 11 , 11 = 0 {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{11,11}=0 shows that Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 2 = m ​ n ​ ( n + 1 ) + n ​ m ​ ( m + 1 ) − 2 ​ m ​ n 2 , {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,2}=\frac{mn(n+1)+nm(m+1)-2mn}{2}, which simplifies to m 3 m^{3} in the special case where m = n m=n . The d = 3 d=3 case is similar. Computing that Tr 𝖱𝖲𝖪 21,111 = 0 , Tr 𝖱𝖲𝖪 12,111 = − 1 , and Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 111,111 = − 3 {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{21,111}=0,{\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{12,111}=-1,\text{and ${\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{111,111}=-3$} implies the following formula, using the values of N σ , π ​ ( m , n , d ) N_{\sigma,\pi}(m,n,d) from Example 3.24 : Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , 3 = ( ( n + 2 3 ) ​ m + ( m + 2 3 ) ​ n − m ​ n ) − 3 ​ ( m 3 ) ​ ( n 3 ) − ( ( m 2 ) ​ ( n 3 ) + ( m 3 ) ​ ( n 2 ) ) . {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,3}=\left({{n+2}\choose 3}m+{{m+2}\choose 3}n-mn\right)-3{m\choose 3}{n\choose 3}-\left({m\choose 2}{n\choose 3}+{m\choose 3}{n\choose 2}\right). When m = n m=n this formula simplifies to Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , m , 3 = − m 2 ​ ( m 4 − 4 ​ m 3 + m 2 − 14 ​ m + 4 ) 12 . {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,m,3}=-\frac{m^{2}(m^{4}-4m^{3}+m^{2}-14m+4)}{12}. Theorem 8.1 and Example suffice to give a simple formula for Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 2 , 2 , d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{2,2,d} , explaining the connection with concentric square numbers mentioned above. In Example 3.25 we computed that 𝖱𝖲𝖪 2 , 2 , d = ( Id 1 ⊕ 4 ​ d ) ⊕ ( ⨁ a = 1 ⌊ d / 2 ⌋ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 a ​ a , a ​ a ⊕ 4 ​ ( d − 2 ​ a ) + δ d , 2 ​ a ) . {\sf RSK}_{2,2,d}=({\rm Id}_{1}^{\oplus 4d})\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{a=1}^{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}{\sf RSK}_{aa,aa}^{\oplus 4(d-2a)+\delta_{d,2a}}\right). Example shows that Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 a ​ a , a ​ a {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{aa,aa} is 0 0 for a a odd and 1 1 for a a even. This yields a trace formula matching the recurrence s d = 4 ​ d + s d − 4 s_{d}=4d+s_{d-4} for the concentric square numbers ( s d ) (s_{d}) : Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 2 , 2 , d = 4 ​ d + ∑ b = 1 ⌊ d / 4 ⌋ ( 4 ​ ( d − 4 ​ b ) + δ d , 4 ​ b ) . {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{2,2,d}=4d+\sum_{b=1}^{\lfloor d/4\rfloor}(4(d-4b)+\delta_{d,4b}). Theorem 8.1 shows that formulas for Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}} determine the growth of Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} except in the “accidental” cases (such as d = 2 d=2 ) where Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d = 0 {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}}=0 . We see lim m , n → ∞ Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d → − ∞ ​ if d = 3 , 4 \lim_{m,n\to\infty}{\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d}\to-\infty\text{ \ if $d=3,4$} and yet lim m , n → ∞ Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d → ∞ ​ if d = 5 . \lim_{m,n\to\infty}{\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d}\to\infty\text{\ if $d=5$}. What are the values (or even the signs) of Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}} for d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 ? In particular, is Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}} always positive (or always negative) for d d sufficiently large? To better investigate Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}} , we record a practical formula that follows immediately from Proposition 4.1 . It allowed us to compute up to d = 11 d=11 : { Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d } d ≥ 1 = { 1 , 0 , − 3 , − 5 , 23 , 96 , − 279 , − 3498,124 , 120819 , 185838 , … } . \{{\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}}\}_{d\geq 1}=\{1,0,-3,-5,23,96,-279,-3498,124,120819,185838,\ldots\}. Corollary 8.4 . Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d = ∑ α ∈ 𝖢𝗈𝗇𝗍 1 d , 1 d ∏ c det α c ​ ( α ) . {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}}=\sum_{\alpha\in{\sf Cont}_{1^{d},1^{d}}}\prod_{c}\det\alpha_{c}(\alpha). Conjecture 8.5 . Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 1 d , 1 d ≠ 0 {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{1^{d},1^{d}}\neq 0 for d ≠ 2 d\neq 2 , i.e., Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} has total degree 2 ​ d 2d for d ≠ 2 d\neq 2 . In order to compute Tr ​ 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d {\rm Tr}\ {\sf RSK}_{m,n,d} more quickly, one must identify more efficient formulas for computing the diagonal entries 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π ​ ( α , α ) {\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}(\alpha,\alpha) . We have such formulas for each of the families from Section 4 (see Proposition 4.1 , Proposition 4.3 , and Proposition ). In these cases we can compute 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π ​ ( α , α ) {\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}(\alpha,\alpha) without determining the entire matrix, and it turns out that 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π ​ ( α , α ) ∈ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } {\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}(\alpha,\alpha)\in\{-1,0,1\} . However, we know of no formula to compute individual entries 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π ​ ( α , α ) {\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}(\alpha,\alpha) for arbitrary weights ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) , and our next result shows that these diagonal entries can be arbitrarily large. Proposition 8.6 . For all N ∈ ℕ N\in{\mathbb{N}} there exist ( σ , π ) (\sigma,\pi) with ( ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) , ℓ ⁡ ( π ) ) = ( 2 , 3 ) (\ell(\sigma),\ell(\pi))=(2,3) and α ∈ 𝖢𝗈𝗇𝗍 σ , π \alpha\in{\sf Cont}_{\sigma,\pi} such that | 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π ​ ( α , α ) | = N |{\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi}(\alpha,\alpha)|=N . Conjecture 8.7 . lim d → ∞ | C d | / d ! = 1 \lim_{d\to\infty}|C_{d}|/d!=1 . By Corollary 8.4 , to prove Conjecture 8.7 it suffices to show the following. Under RSK insertion of almost any permutation matrix (i.e., Schensted insertion), some label in the first row of the partially completed P P tableau gets bumped to a column strictly to the left. Since 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ , π {\sf RSK}_{\sigma,\pi} and 𝖱𝖲𝖪 σ ~ , π ~ {\sf RSK}_{\widetilde{\sigma},\widetilde{\pi}} are similar if and only if their inverses are, a result analogous to Theorem 3.22 holds for the matrices 𝖱𝖲𝖪 m , n , d − 1 {\sf RSK}^{-1}_{m,n,d} . This implies similar results on the trace, determinant, and so on. Instructions for reporting errors We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the methods listed below: Click the "Report Issue" ( ) button, located in the page header. Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. Our team has already identified the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability should not be a barrier to accessing research.
